<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> Every regular language over a finite alphabet has a generalized regular expression of star height at most thirteen over that same alphabet. We prove this uniform bound by representing finite monoid computations as affine updates and recovering them through twelve successive split constructions.

- **claim-main-height-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every finite alphabet $\Sigma$ and every regular language $L\subseteq\Sigma^*$, $$h_\Sigma(L)\le 13.$$
  - Explanation: Every regular language over any finite alphabet has a complement-allowed regular expression whose star nesting is at most thirteen, uniformly in the alphabet and the automaton.
- **claim-split-identity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-split)): Let $D'\subseteq D\subseteq E$, with the same deterministic updates on the common episodes. Suppose languages $P_x,Q_y$, indexed by $x,y\in S$, satisfy:* 1. *If $w\in D^*$ and $uv$ is a prefix of $w$, with $u\in P_x$ and $v\in Q_y$, then $w$ has a first episode $e$, $uv=e$, and $G_e(x)=y$.* 2. *For every $e\in D\setminus D'$ and $x\in S$, there is a split $e=uv$ with $u\in P_x$ and $v\in Q_{G_e(x)}$.* *Write $W_{x,y}$ for the exact graph test on $(D')^*$. Then the exact graph test on $D^*$ is $$\label{eq:split} \begin{split} D^*\cap\Bigl( W_{x,y}\ \cup\ &\bigl(\,\bigcup_a W_{x,a}P_a\,\bigr) \bigl(\,\bigcup_{b,c}Q_bW_{b,c}P_c\,\bigr)^*\\[-2pt] &\hspace{35mm}\cdot \bigl(\,\bigcup_r Q_rW_{r,y}\,\bigr)\Bigr). \end{split}$$ In particular, if $D,P_x,Q_y$ have heights at most $1,0,1$, respectively, and all $W_{x,y}$ have height at most $H$, the new graph tests have height at most $\max\{2,H+1\}$.
  - Explanation: If prefix and suffix tests are sound for every independent successful pair and available at every non-skipped episode, the graph test on episode sequences is one union-concatenation-star expression over the skipped tests. Each split raises the height by at most one.
- **claim-affine-recovery** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-affine-recovery)): Suppose the deterministic update on each episode is $v\mapsto vA_e+b_e$ on a fixed finite vector space. Exact graph tests for the composed affine update on all vectors give, by finite Boolean operations, the graph tests for the composition of the $A_e$.
  - Explanation: Graph tests for composed affine updates yield, by finite Boolean operations, graph tests for the composition of the linear parts. The linear part is read off as the difference between the values at a vector and at zero.
- **claim-prediction-trial** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-prediction)): There is a deterministic shift $\beta\in U$, defined from the actual trial word and its prediction, such that every ideally eligible pair satisfies $y_j=x_jT+\beta$. A perfectly predicted trial has an eligible block for every possible input vector.
  - Explanation: All ideally eligible blocks of a trial share one deterministic shift, and a perfectly predicted trial has an eligible block for every input vector. Two eligible blocks force equality of the whole product with the predicted product.
- **claim-tag-clock** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-clock)): Let $\Lambda$ be a nonempty finite set, let $m=\|\Lambda\|$, let $\mathcal R$ be any finite set, and let $B>0$. There are a positive integer $n$, sets $I_\alpha,J_\alpha\subseteq\mathbb Z/n\mathbb Z$ for $\alpha\in\Lambda$, a set $W\subseteq\mathbb Z/n\mathbb Z$, and residues $v_p\in\mathbb Z/n\mathbb Z$ for $p\in\mathcal R$, with the following properties.* 1. *Every prime factor of $n$ exceeds $B$.* 2. *For every $\ell\in\mathbb Z/n\mathbb Z$, the directed graph $$\mathcal E_\ell =\{(\alpha,\beta)\in\Lambda^2: \ell\in I_\alpha+J_\beta\}$$ is either contained in the diagonal $\{(\alpha,\alpha):\alpha\in\Lambda\}$ or consists of exactly one ordered pair $(\alpha,\beta)$ with $\alpha\ne\beta$.* 3. *If $\ell\notin W$, then $\mathcal E_\ell$ is the full diagonal.* 4. *For every $u\in\mathbb Z/n\mathbb Z$, $$\left\|\left\{(p,p')\in\mathcal R^2: p\ne p',\quad u+v_{p'}-v_p\in W\right\}\right\| \le c_{\mathrm{cl}}\log(2m), \label{eq:clock-roster}$$ where the absolute constant $c_{\mathrm{cl}}=1\,300\,000$ is valid.* *In particular, $c_{\mathrm{cl}}$ is independent of $B$, $m$, and $\|\mathcal R\|$.
  - Explanation: A finite cyclic residue clock makes independently chosen tags either agree or collide on a single off-diagonal pair. Away from a bad set the full diagonal is available, and the number of spoiled ordered roster pairs grows only logarithmically in the tag count.
- **claim-local-markers** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-markers)): For integers $d\ge2$ and $K\ge1$ there are an integer $r\ge K$ and a fixed, translation-equivariant marking rule on two-sided words with the following properties.* 1. *Distinct markers have distance at least $d$.* 2. *Whether $x$ is marked depends only on the letters in $[x-r,x+r)$.* 3. *If no marker lies in $[z-d,z+d]$, the block $[z,z+K)$ has a period strictly less than $d$.* *Consequently a marker test whose inspection interval is present in a finite word is independent of its two-sided extension.
  - Explanation: A translation-equivariant marking rule with finite inspection radius places markers far apart, and a marker-free window forces a short period on the block after it.
- **claim-periodic-overlap** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-periodic-overlap)): Two two-sided words of respective periods $a,b$ which agree on $ab$ consecutive letter positions agree everywhere. In particular:* 1. *If $K-w>d^2$, length-$K$ blocks starting at a finite nonempty set of positions of diameter at most $w$, each having a period below $d$, have one common two-sided periodic extension. The word agrees with this extension on the whole union of these blocks.* 2. *Use the rule of Lemma <a href="#lem:markers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:markers">8</a> with $K>3d^2$. If $H\ge d$ and $[z-H,z+H]$ contains no marker, all the blocks starting at integer positions from $z-H+d$ through $z+H-d$ belong to one common two-sided periodic word of some period below $d$. The original word agrees with it throughout $[z-H+d,z+H-d+K)$.* *All statements apply to a finite word when the indicated blocks and marker inspection intervals are available.
  - Explanation: Short-period blocks that overlap sufficiently share one two-sided periodic extension. A marker-free stretch therefore lies inside a single periodic word.
- **claim-search-crossings** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-search-crossings)): Let $I$ be an interval of integer centers of diameter $D$.* 1. *Suppose distinct points of $S$ are at least $d$ apart. If $h\ge0$ and $D+2h<d$, at most $2(2h+1)$ centers $z\in I$ satisfy $$f_L(z+e)\ne f_L(z)\quad\text{for some integer }\|e\|\le h.$$ This estimate is independent of $L$.* 2. *Let $h\ge1$. If $D<2L+2$, at most $h$ centers $z\in I$ satisfy $$S\cap[z-L,z+L]=\varnothing, \qquad S\cap(z+L,z+L+h]\ne\varnothing.$$ This estimate does not require a separation hypothesis on $S$.* *If centers are indexed by ordered pairs $(p,p')$, $p\ne p'$, as $z=z_0+p-p'$, and all their ordered nonzero differences are distinct, the same bounds count ordered pairs.
  - Explanation: Over a range of centers, only a bounded number of first-marker searches change under small shifts, or have an empty search with a marker just beyond the right margin.
- **claim-prefix-code** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-prefix-code)): The language $E$ is a prefix code. Its end decisions have the same values in an episode considered alone and in any word containing that episode at the same position.
  - Explanation: The language of complete episodes is a prefix code, and its end decisions are the same whether the episode stands alone or sits inside a larger word.
- **claim-shared-anchor** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-shared-anchor)): Let a word begin with a complete episode $e$ at $s$, with origin $t$, anchor $q$, and end $b_0=q+B_{\rm tail}$. Let $s<k<b_0$ be a cut, and let $[k,b)$ be any proposed suffix factor of that word; its endpoint $b$ may be before or after $b_0$. Let $\mathcal T$ be a finite nonempty set of candidate origins such that $$t\in\mathcal T,\qquad \operatorname{diam}\mathcal T\le w_E.$$ Assume that every candidate’s end-search inspections and seed block lie inside both $[s,b_0)$ and $[k,b)$. Suppose the proposed suffix requires* 1. *equality of $\eta(\tau)$ for all $\tau\in\mathcal T$;* 2. *the exact end rule of Definition <a href="#def:episodes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:episodes">11</a> at any one reference $\tau_0\in\mathcal T$, with anchor $b-B_{\rm tail}$.* *Then $b=b_0$, and the reference anchor is $q$. Moreover, if $\eta$ is stable within $w_E$ at $t$ and all the candidate data are visible in the actual suffix $[k,b_0)$, these two requirements hold for that suffix and every choice of reference.
  - Explanation: A suffix that requires equal end-marker values over a stable candidate origin set, and the exact end rule at one reference, must end exactly at the true episode end with the true anchor.
- **claim-decoded-end** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-decoded-end)): Let $e,k,[k,b),t,q$ be as in Lemma <a href="#lem:shared-anchor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:shared-anchor">13</a>, and let $\mathcal T$ be any finite candidate set containing $t$, with no diameter restriction. Suppose its candidates are tested by bounded predicates which do not use an anchor, all their inspection intervals lie inside both the actual episode and the proposed suffix, and the true origin passes its predicate. If exactly one candidate passes, it is the true origin. Applying the exact end rule at that decoded origin, with its search and seed data also present in both factors, forces the proposed suffix to end at $q+B_{\rm tail}$ without any stability assumption.
  - Explanation: If bounded anchor-free predicates leave exactly one passing candidate origin, that candidate is the true origin, and the exact end rule there fixes the suffix end without any stability assumption.
- **claim-main-split** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-main-split)): The languages $P_x^{(1)},Q_y^{(1)}$ satisfy Lemma <a href="#lem:split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:split">3</a> on $D=E$, with updates $F_{1,e}$ and skipped class $E\setminus S_1$.
  - Explanation: The prediction-trial prefix and suffix languages are sound on every episode and available outside the skipped class, so the split identity applies with the first affine update.
- **claim-periodic-split** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-periodic-split)): The second pair of languages satisfies Lemma <a href="#lem:split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:split">3</a> on $D=E\setminus S_1$, with updates $F_{2,e}$ and skipped class $$D_0=E\setminus(S_1\cup S_2).$$
  - Explanation: The periodic-window languages are sound on the episodes left by the first split and available on those with a long marker-free window, leaving a residual class.
- **claim-witness-coverage** (classification, [source](#stmt-lem-witness-coverage)): Every episode of $D_0$ satisfies at least one of $A_0,\ldots,A_4$.
  - Explanation: Every episode not handled by either outer split exhibits at least one of five explicit failure witnesses.
- **claim-total-tables** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-total-tables)): The tables $\Phi_i$ exist for $0\le i\le4$. Every $g_{i+1,e}$ just defined is deterministic affine, independent of split success and domain restrictions. Its linear part $L_{i+1,e}$ satisfies $$\label{eq:basis-recovery} [v,1_M]L_{i+1,e}=[g_{i,e}(v),1_M].$$ The state spaces, tree sizes, and aligned trial counts depend only on the preceding state sizes and $M$, not on the length or complexity of a table formula.
  - Explanation: Total suffix tables exist at every stage, even for hypothetical prefix products. The new update is deterministic affine and its linear part recovers the previous stage update on basis states.
- **claim-time-decoder** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-time-decoder)): Paired with a genuine stencil prefix in the promised domain $D_i$, a successful primary or secondary decoder returns the actual offset and consumes exactly the first episode. On its respective full set, all later guards pass at every stencil cut, and at most $C_i$ offsets fail uniqueness. Thus every required kind has a successfully decoded copy.
  - Explanation: A successful decoder identifies the true cut offset and consumes exactly the first episode. Only boundedly many offsets fail uniqueness, so each required kind keeps a decodable copy.
- **claim-bad-histories** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-bad-histories)): Fix one episode and the actual product tuples on all its aligned trial intervals. Fewer than $R$ leaf histories have no perfectly predicted trial, including infeasible histories. Along the true path, fewer than $R$ nodes have a wrong proposed measurement value whose suffix continuation yields such a history.
  - Explanation: Because predictions exhaust all tuples across the leaves of a trial, fewer than the allocated number of leaf histories have no perfectly predicted trial. Fewer than that many true-path nodes can carry a harmful wrong measurement.
- **claim-aligned-stage** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-aligned-stage)): Assume the entry promise, the table and placement properties, and <a href="#eq:ambiguity-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ambiguity-bound">[eq:ambiguity-bound]</a>, with more than $C_i$ copies per kind. Exact graph tests for $g_{i+1}$ on $D_{i+1}^*$ yield graph tests for $g_i$ on $D_i^*$ by two uses of Lemma <a href="#lem:split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:split">3</a> and finite Boolean operations. All required hypotheses hold at the next residual stage, and $D_5=\varnothing$.
  - Explanation: One aligned stage turns graph tests for the next update on the shrunken domain into graph tests for the current update by two uses of the split identity, and the final domain is empty.
- **claim-parameters** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-parameters)): For every fixed nonempty finite alphabet, finite monoid $M$, and finite representation space $V$, all parameters in the two outer splits and the five aligned stages can be chosen to satisfy their stated hypotheses. More precisely, the stencils are nonempty, have more than $C_i$ copies of each required kind, and satisfy $$\#\{(p,p')\in\mathcal P_i^2:p\ne p',\ A_i^{\Gamma_i}(t+p-p',q)\}\le C_i \qquad(0\le i\le4)$$ on every complete episode. Write $N_i=\|\mathcal P_i\|$. In order, these bounds concern first-clock or prediction failure, inner-search instability, second-clock failure, a right-margin marker, and end-search instability. The constants can be taken to be $$\begin{aligned} C_0&=K+c_{\rm cl}\log(2m_1),\label{eq:parameter-c0}\\ C_1&=10\|\mathcal Z\|N_0^4(2R_1+3),\label{eq:parameter-c1}\\ C_2&=N_0^4N_1^4c_{\rm cl}\log(2m_2),\label{eq:parameter-c2}\\ C_3&=10\|\mathcal Z\|(2R_2+3)\prod_{j<3}N_j^4, \label{eq:parameter-c3}\\ C_4&=10(2w_E+3)\prod_{j<4}N_j^4. \label{eq:parameter-c4} \end{aligned}$$ Here $K=\|M\|^{\|V\|^2+1}$, $m_1,m_2$ are the respective full tag alphabet sizes, and $c_{\rm cl}$ is the absolute constant of Lemma <a href="#lem:clock" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:clock">7</a>; logarithms are natural. Also, $\operatorname{diam}\mathcal P_i<w_E$ for $i<4$. All measurements, trials, and inspections have the chronological and reading-side access required in Lemmas <a href="#lem:main-split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:main-split">15</a>, <a href="#lem:periodic-split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:periodic-split">16</a>, and <a href="#lem:aligned-stage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:aligned-stage">21</a>. Every bounded control, including every control at a false candidate origin, lies inside the true first episode. The same choices satisfy the end margins of Section <a href="#sec:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:geometry">4</a>.
  - Explanation: All finite parameters of the two outer splits and five aligned stages can be chosen in a compatible order, with explicit ambiguity constants and with every bounded control inside the true first episode.
- **claim-periodic-template** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-template)): Fix words $a,b,c\in\Sigma^*$, with $b\ne\epsilon$. If $S\subseteq\mathbb N$ is ultimately periodic, then $$\{ab^jc:j\in S\}$$ has a generalized expression of height at most one. The same bound holds for a finite union of such languages and finite languages.* *Moreover, on the template $ab^jc$, every fixed finite calculation from the following data is ultimately periodic as a function of $j$:* 1. *letters, and their availability, at offsets in a fixed finite set from the argument start or end;* 2. *geometric validity and $M$-products of intervals whose endpoints belong to such finite offset sets;* 3. *the argument length, or a fixed offset from it, modulo any one of finitely many fixed moduli.* *Finite branching among fixed endpoint choices and a fixed finite recursion of table calculations are allowed.
  - Explanation: Ultimately periodic exponent sets on a three-factor template give height at most one, and finite calculations of local data on the template are ultimately periodic in the exponent.
- **claim-component-cost** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-component-cost)): Every single-episode domain used in the construction, including $E$, $S_1,S_2,D_i,D_i'$ and the full sets, has height at most one. Every prefix component $P_x$ has height zero and every suffix component $Q_y$ has height at most one. These are expressions over the original alphabet, including when a standalone suffix’s guessed metadata or origin is false.
  - Explanation: Each single-episode domain has height at most one, prefix components are finite, and suffix components have height at most one over the original alphabet.
- **claim-final-suffix** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-final-suffix)): For every $m\in M$, the language $$\{f\in F:T_f=m\}$$ has height at most one. Every word of $\Sigma^*$ belongs to $E^*F$.
  - Explanation: The words with no complete episode prefix and a given monoid product form a height-one language, and every word splits as episodes followed by such a remainder.

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-clock",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\Lambda$ be a nonempty finite set, let $m=|\\Lambda|$, let $\\mathcal R$ be any finite set, and let $B>0$. There are a positive integer $n$, sets $I_\\alpha,J_\\alpha\\subseteq\\mathbb Z/n\\mathbb Z$ for $\\alpha\\in\\Lambda$, a set $W\\subseteq\\mathbb Z/n\\mathbb Z$, and residues $v_p\\in\\mathbb Z/n\\mathbb Z$ for $p\\in\\mathcal R$, with the following properties.*\n\n1.  *Every prime factor of $n$ exceeds $B$.*\n\n2.  *For every $\\ell\\in\\mathbb Z/n\\mathbb Z$, the directed graph $$\\mathcal E_\\ell\n     =\\{(\\alpha,\\beta)\\in\\Lambda^2:\n                     \\ell\\in I_\\alpha+J_\\beta\\}$$ is either contained in the diagonal $\\{(\\alpha,\\alpha):\\alpha\\in\\Lambda\\}$ or consists of exactly one ordered pair $(\\alpha,\\beta)$ with $\\alpha\\ne\\beta$.*\n\n3.  *If $\\ell\\notin W$, then $\\mathcal E_\\ell$ is the full diagonal.*\n\n\n4.  *For every $u\\in\\mathbb Z/n\\mathbb Z$, $$\\left|\\left\\{(p,p')\\in\\mathcal R^2:\n           p\\ne p',\\quad u+v_{p'}-v_p\\in W\\right\\}\\right|\n     \\le c_{\\mathrm{cl}}\\log(2m),\n     \\label{eq:clock-roster}$$ where the absolute constant $c_{\\mathrm{cl}}=1\\,300\\,000$ is valid.*\n\n*In particular, $c_{\\mathrm{cl}}$ is independent of $B$, $m$, and $|\\mathcal R|$.",
        "source_id": "PM-134.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-clock",
          "exact_quote": "Let $\\Lambda$ be a nonempty finite set, let $m=|\\Lambda|$, let $\\mathcal R$ be any finite set, and let $B>0$.           "source_id": "PM-134.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $\\Lambda$ be a nonempty finite set, let $m=|\\Lambda|$, let $\\mathcal R$ be any finite set, and let $B>0$. There are a positive integer $n$, sets $I_\\alpha,J_\\alpha\\subseteq\\mathbb Z/n\\mathbb Z$ for $\\alpha\\in\\Lambda$, a set $W\\subseteq\\mathbb Z/n\\mathbb Z$, and residues $v_p\\in\\mathbb Z/n\\mathbb Z$ for $p\\in\\mathcal R$, with the following properties.*\n\n1.  *Every prime factor of $n$ exceeds $B$.*\n\n2.  *For every $\\ell\\in\\mathbb Z/n\\mathbb Z$, the directed graph $$\\mathcal E_\\ell\n     =\\{(\\alpha,\\beta)\\in\\Lambda^2:\n                     \\ell\\in I_\\alpha+J_\\beta\\}$$ is either contained in the diagonal $\\{(\\alpha,\\alpha):\\alpha\\in\\Lambda\\}$ or consists of exactly one ordered pair $(\\alpha,\\beta)$ with $\\alpha\\ne\\beta$.*\n\n3.  *If $\\ell\\notin W$, then $\\mathcal E_\\ell$ is the full diagonal.*\n\n\n4.  *For every $u\\in\\mathbb Z/n\\mathbb Z$, $$\\left|\\left\\{(p,p')\\in\\mathcal R^2:\n           p\\ne p',\\quad u+v_{p'}-v_p\\in W\\right\\}\\right|\n     \\le c_{\\mathrm{cl}}\\log(2m),\n     \\label{eq:clock-roster}$$ where the absolute constant $c_{\\mathrm{cl}}=1\\,300\\,000$ is valid.*\n\n*In particular, $c_{\\mathrm{cl}}$ is independent of $B$, $m$, and $|\\mathcal R|$.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-markers",
        "exact_quote": "For integers $d\\ge2$ and $K\\ge1$ there are an integer $r\\ge K$ and a fixed, translation-equivariant marking rule on two-sided words with the following properties.*\n\n1.  *Distinct markers have distance at least $d$.*\n\n2.  *Whether $x$ is marked depends only on the letters in $[x-r,x+r)$.*\n\n3.  *If no marker lies in $[z-d,z+d]$, the block $[z,z+K)$ has a period strictly less than $d$.*\n\n*Consequently a marker test whose inspection interval is present in a finite word is independent of its two-sided extension.",
        "source_id": "PM-134.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-markers",
          "exact_quote": "For integers $d\\ge2$ and $K\\ge1$ there are an integer $r\\ge K$ and a fixed, translation-equivariant marking rule on two-sided words with the following properties.*\n\n1.  *Distinct markers have distance at least $d$.*\n\n2.  *Whether $x$ is marked depends only on the letters in $[x-r,x+r)$.*\n\n3.  *If no marker lies in $[z-d,z+d]$, the block $[z,z+K)$ has a period strictly less than $d$.*\n\n*Consequently a marker test whose inspection interval is present in a finite word is independent of its two-sided extension.",
          "source_id": "PM-134.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For integers $d\\ge2$ and $K\\ge1$ there are an integer $r\\ge K$ and a fixed, translation-equivariant marking rule on two-sided words with the following properties.*\n\n1.  *Distinct markers have distance at least $d$.*\n\n2.  *Whether $x$ is marked depends only on the letters in $[x-r,x+r)$.*\n\n3.  *If no marker lies in $[z-d,z+d]$, the block $[z,z+K)$ has a period strictly less than $d$.*\n\n*Consequently a marker test whose inspection interval is present in a finite word is independent of its two-sided extension.",
      "explanation": "A translation-equivariant marking rule with finite inspection radius places markers far apart, and a marker-free window forces a short period on the block after it.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-periodic-overlap",
        "exact_quote": "Two two-sided words of respective periods $a,b$ which agree on $ab$ consecutive letter positions agree everywhere. In particular:*\n\n1.  *If $K-w>d^2$, length-$K$ blocks starting at a finite nonempty set of positions of diameter at most $w$, each having a period below $d$, have one common two-sided periodic extension. The word agrees with this extension on the whole union of these blocks.*\n\n2.  *Use the rule of Lemma <a href=\"#lem:markers\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:markers\">8</a> with $K>3d^2$. If $H\\ge d$ and $[z-H,z+H]$ contains no marker, all the blocks starting at integer positions from $z-H+d$ through $z+H-d$ belong to one common two-sided periodic word of some period below $d$. The original word agrees with it throughout $[z-H+d,z+H-d+K)$.*\n\n*All statements apply to a finite word when the indicated blocks and marker inspection intervals are available.",

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-search-crossings",
        "exact_quote": "Let $I$ be an interval of integer centers of diameter $D$.*\n\n1.  *Suppose distinct points of $S$ are at least $d$ apart. If $h\\ge0$ and $D+2h<d$, at most $2(2h+1)$ centers $z\\in I$ satisfy $$f_L(z+e)\\ne f_L(z)\\quad\\text{for some integer }|e|\\le h.$$ This estimate is independent of $L$.*\n\n2.  *Let $h\\ge1$. If $D<2L+2$, at most $h$ centers $z\\in I$ satisfy $$S\\cap[z-L,z+L]=\\varnothing,\n     \\qquad S\\cap(z+L,z+L+h]\\ne\\varnothing.$$ This estimate does not require a separation hypothesis on $S$.*\n\n*If centers are indexed by ordered pairs $(p,p')$, $p\\ne p'$, as $z=z_0+p-p'$, and all their ordered nonzero differences are distinct, the same bounds count ordered pairs.",

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-shared-anchor",
        "exact_quote": "Let a word begin with a complete episode $e$ at $s$, with origin $t$, anchor $q$, and end $b_0=q+B_{\\rm tail}$. Let $s<k<b_0$ be a cut, and let $[k,b)$ be any proposed suffix factor of that word; its endpoint $b$ may be before or after $b_0$. Let $\\mathcal T$ be a finite nonempty set of candidate origins such that $$t\\in\\mathcal T,\\qquad \\operatorname{diam}\\mathcal T\\le w_E.$$ Assume that every candidate’s end-search inspections and seed block lie inside both $[s,b_0)$ and $[k,b)$. Suppose the proposed suffix requires*\n\n1.  *equality of $\\eta(\\tau)$ for all $\\tau\\in\\mathcal T$;*\n\n2.  *the exact end rule of Definition <a href=\"#def:episodes\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"def:episodes\">11</a> at any one reference $\\tau_0\\in\\mathcal T$, with anchor $b-B_{\\rm tail}$.*\n\n*Then $b=b_0$, and the reference anchor is $q$. Moreover, if $\\eta$ is stable within $w_E$ at $t$ and all the candidate data are visible in the actual suffix $[k,b_0)$, these two requirements hold for that suffix and every choice of reference.",

        "exact_quote": "Let $e,k,[k,b),t,q$ be as in Lemma <a href=\"#lem:shared-anchor\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:shared-anchor\">13</a>, and let $\\mathcal T$ be any finite candidate set containing $t$, with no diameter restriction.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-parameters",
        "exact_quote": "For every fixed nonempty finite alphabet, finite monoid $M$, and finite representation space $V$, all parameters in the two outer splits and the five aligned stages can be chosen to satisfy their stated hypotheses. More precisely, the stencils are nonempty, have more than $C_i$ copies of each required kind, and satisfy $$\\#\\{(p,p')\\in\\mathcal P_i^2:p\\ne p',\\\n       A_i^{\\Gamma_i}(t+p-p',q)\\}\\le C_i\n       \\qquad(0\\le i\\le4)$$ on every complete episode. Write $N_i=|\\mathcal P_i|$. In order, these bounds concern first-clock or prediction failure, inner-search instability, second-clock failure, a right-margin marker, and end-search instability. The constants can be taken to be $$\\begin{aligned}\n C_0&=K+c_{\\rm cl}\\log(2m_1),\\label{eq:parameter-c0}\\\\\n C_1&=10|\\mathcal Z|N_0^4(2R_1+3),\\label{eq:parameter-c1}\\\\\n C_2&=N_0^4N_1^4c_{\\rm cl}\\log(2m_2),\\label{eq:parameter-c2}\\\\\n C_3&=10|\\mathcal Z|(2R_2+3)\\prod_{j<3}N_j^4,\n       \\label{eq:parameter-c3}\\\\\n C_4&=10(2w_E+3)\\prod_{j<4}N_j^4.\n       \\label{eq:parameter-c4}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Here $K=|M|^{|V|^2+1}$, $m_1,m_2$ are the respective full tag alphabet sizes, and $c_{\\rm cl}$ is the absolute constant of Lemma <a href=\"#lem:clock\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:clock\">7</a>; logarithms are natural. Also, $\\operatorname{diam}\\mathcal P_i<w_E$ for $i<4$. All measurements, trials, and inspections have the chronological and reading-side access required in Lemmas <a href=\"#lem:main-split\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:main-split\">15</a>, <a href=\"#lem:periodic-split\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:periodic-split\">16</a>, and <a href=\"#lem:aligned-stage\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:aligned-stage\">21</a>. Every bounded control, including every control at a false candidate origin, lies inside the true first episode. The same choices satisfy the end margins of Section <a href=\"#sec:geometry\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"sec:geometry\">4</a>.",
        "source_id": "PM-134.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-template",
        "exact_quote": "Fix words $a,b,c\\in\\Sigma^*$, with $b\\ne\\epsilon$. If $S\\subseteq\\mathbb N$ is ultimately periodic, then $$\\{ab^jc:j\\in S\\}$$ has a generalized expression of height at most one. The same bound holds for a finite union of such languages and finite languages.*\n\n*Moreover, on the template $ab^jc$, every fixed finite calculation from the following data is ultimately periodic as a function of $j$:*\n\n1.  *letters, and their availability, at offsets in a fixed finite set from the argument start or end;*\n\n2.  *geometric validity and $M$-products of intervals whose endpoints belong to such finite offset sets;*\n\n3.  *the argument length, or a fixed offset from it, modulo any one of finitely many fixed moduli.*\n\n*Finite branching among fixed endpoint choices and a fixed finite recursion of table calculations are allowed.",
        "source_id": "PM-134.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-template",

          "source_id": "PM-134.1"
        }
      ],

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-component-cost",
        "exact_quote": "Every single-episode domain used in the construction, including $E$, $S_1,S_2,D_i,D_i'$ and the full sets, has height at most one. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **prefix code (episodes)** ([source](#pm-h-7)): $$ A right action on a vector space is denoted $v\mapsto vT_{a,b}$. The vector space with basis indexed by $M$ has the faithful action $e_m d=e_{md}$; the image of $e_{1_M}$ identifies $d$. All vector spaces used below are finite and are over $\mathbf F_2$. For a DFA, take $M$ to be its monoid of state transformations induced by words, including the identity. The product of a word determines acceptance. It is therefore enough to construct uniformly bounded-height tests for each value of $T$. The theorem will follow by a finite accepting union. <div class="remark"> *Remark 2* (A DFA-state representation). One may instead begin with the basis indexed by DFA states and query only the image of the initial-state vector to test acceptance. That image need not identify the monoid product. The main proof uses the basis indexed by $M$; the alternative and its final acceptance query are described at the end of Section <a href="#sec:compilation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:compilation">8</a>. In either case interval predictions and the later work registers still take values in $M$. </div> A *prefix code* $E$ is a set of nonempty words, no one a proper prefix of another.
- **period of a finite block** ([source](#pm-h-23)): A positive integer $d$ is a *period* of a finite block if letters at positions $d$ apart agree whenever both positions belong to the block.
- **ideal eligibility** ([source](#stmt-def-eligibility)): Block $j$ is *ideally eligible* if $$x_jT_*=y_j,\qquad T_{z_{i-1},z_i}=p_i\ (1\le i<j),\qquad T_{z_j,C}=p_{j+1}\cdots p_{J+1}.$$ A trial is *perfectly predicted* if every interval product is its corresponding $p_i$.
- **complete episode and anchor** ([source](#stmt-def-episodes)): Fix a positive integer $B_{\rm tail}$. With all required bounded data present, the *anchor* at origin $t$ is defined as follows: 1. if $\eta(t)\ne\bot$, the anchor is $q=\eta(t)$; 2. if $\eta(t)=\bot$, the anchor is the first letter position $q\ge z_\bullet(t)+K_\bullet$ whose letter differs from $\pi_t(q)$. If no such letter is present, no anchor has yet been found. An episode beginning at $s$ ends exactly at $q+B_{\rm tail}$. The language $E$ consists of the nonempty words which, with $s=0$, contain the required search and seed data, have an anchor under this rule, and end exactly there. In the markerless case the tail includes the mismatch letter at $q$, since $B_{\rm tail}\ge1$.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-split** [claim](#stmt-lem-split) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-split): Parse by the prefix code, induct through the starred factors for soundness, split every non-skipped episode for completeness, count heights. -> expression for the graph test on D^* with height cost
- **step-affine** [claim](#stmt-lem-affine-recovery) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-affine-recovery): Compose affine maps and read the linear part from values at a vector and at zero. -> graph tests for composed linear parts
- **step-prediction** [claim](#stmt-lem-prediction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-prediction): Two eligible blocks force the whole product to equal the predicted product, giving one shift. -> deterministic shift and eligible block for perfect trials
- **step-clock** [claim](#stmt-lem-clock) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-clock): Build cyclic coordinates from partitions, two batch types and a forest net, then merge by the Chinese remainder isomorphism. -> residue sets, bad set and roster residues
- **step-markers** [claim](#stmt-lem-markers) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-markers): Install markers stage by stage over the aperiodic blocks, preserving separation and locality. -> marking rule with finite radius
- **step-overlap** [claim](#stmt-lem-periodic-overlap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-periodic-overlap): Use the product of two periods as a common period to extend agreement everywhere. -> common periodic extension
- **step-search** [claim](#stmt-lem-search-crossings) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-search-crossings): Charge each change of a first-marker search to one marker near an endpoint. -> bounds on unstable centers
- **step-prefix-code** [claim](#stmt-lem-prefix-code) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-prefix-code): Compare two episodes with a common start and show equal anchors and equal lengths. -> E is a prefix code
- **step-shared-anchor** [claim](#stmt-lem-shared-anchor) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-shared-anchor): Merge the seed blocks of all candidates into one periodic word and locate the first mismatch. -> true anchor and exact suffix end
- **step-decoded-end** [claim](#stmt-lem-decoded-end) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-decoded-end): Uniqueness identifies the true origin, then apply the shared-anchor lemma at the singleton set. -> exact end without stability
- **step-main-split** [claim](#stmt-lem-main-split) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-main-split): Soundness from the end guard, clock alternative and prediction lemma; availability from a perfect trial and a good clock residue. -> split for the first affine update
- **step-periodic-split** [claim](#stmt-lem-periodic-split) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-periodic-split): Use a long periodic run, stabilised monoid powers and coprime displacements to realise every second-clock residue. -> split for the lifted update
- **step-witness** [claim](#stmt-lem-witness-coverage) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-witness-coverage): Case analysis on the failure of the two availability conditions. -> five witnesses
- **step-tables** [claim](#stmt-lem-total-tables) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-total-tables): Define tables for every hypothetical prefix product and extend the terminal map linearly on a basis. -> total tables and linear recovery of the previous update
- **step-decoder** [claim](#stmt-lem-time-decoder) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-time-decoder): Test all candidate origins, eliminate later witnesses, require uniqueness, and count ambiguous offsets by injection. -> decodable copy of each kind
- **step-bad-histories** [claim](#stmt-lem-bad-histories) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-bad-histories): Pigeonhole over a trial whose predictions exhaust all tuples, then inject ambiguous nodes into the bad set. -> bounds on bad histories
- **step-aligned** [claim](#stmt-lem-aligned-stage) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-aligned-stage): Secondary split then primary split on the same update, followed by affine recovery on basis states. -> graph tests for the current stage update
- **step-parameters** [claim](#stmt-prop-parameters) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-parameters): Fix finite kinds first, then copy counts, clocks, markers and positions in a compatible order, and finally the margin lengths. -> consistent schedules with ambiguity bounds and margins
- **step-template** [claim](#stmt-lem-template) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-template): Express ultimately periodic exponent sets with one star and show finite calculations are ultimately periodic. -> height-one expressions
- **step-component** [claim](#stmt-prop-component-cost) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-component-cost): Case on the end-rule origin, reduce each case to a template, and apply the template lemma. -> component expressions of height at most one
- **step-final-suffix** [claim](#stmt-lem-final-suffix) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-final-suffix): Finite short words plus periodic templates for long remainders, then peel complete episodes. -> height-one remainder test and factorization
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-final-suffix-2): Apply the aligned stages backward, then the periodic and main splits, count twelve split steps, and combine with the remainder test by finite union. -> height at most thirteen

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-split-height**: One application of the split identity turns skip graph tests of height at most H into graph tests of height at most max{2,H+1}, when the domain, prefix and suffix components have heights at most 1, 0 and 1 respectively.
- **inv-twelve-splits**: Starting from height zero on the empty residual domain, twelve successive split applications, each changing a skip bound H to at most max{2,H+1}, give the bound thirteen.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-minimal-height**: Using the height count as an algorithm that returns the minimum generalized star height of a given regular language. -> The recurrence only certifies a uniform upper bound for the construction; it says nothing about the minimum height of a particular language.
- **anti-compact-expressions**: Expecting the construction to produce compact regular expressions whose size is controlled. -> The result bounds only nesting of stars; expression length is unrestricted and the finite objects involved can be extremely large.
- **anti-height-one**: Assuming that generalized star height one always suffices for regular languages. -> The theorem only gives a uniform finite bound; whether height one suffices remains open.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `algorithmic`. The full construction is astronomically large and is not executed here. Only the finite height bookkeeping of the split recursion is checked: iterating the map H to max{2,H+1} for a given number of split applications, starting from a given height.

```python
# INPUTS: two integers on stdin separated by whitespace: starting height H0 >= 0 and number of split applications n >= 0.
# PRECONDITIONS: H0 and n are non-negative integers of modest size.
# OUTPUTS: one integer on stdout, the height bound after n applications of the split recurrence.
# TIME: O(n) integer operations.
# SPACE: O(1) integers.
# ARITHMETIC: exact Python integers, no rounding.
# FAILURE: malformed input raises an exception and the process exits nonzero.
import sys


def step(h):
    return max(2, h + 1)


def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    h = int(data[0])
    n = int(data[1])
    for _ in range(n):
        h = step(h)
    print(h)


main()
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No program is specified. The result bounds the star height of expressions for a regular language, and the paper places no limit on expression length, so it gives no practical compilation target for software. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper contains no dynamics or simulation content. Its episodes and trials are proof devices for parsing finite words, not simulation steps. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper says outright that it does not determine the minimum generalized height of a given language. A tool that computes or optimizes star height therefore cannot be built from it. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The proof builds expressions out of finite objects whose sizes are enormous and are never given explicitly. Only the nesting count matters, so the paper states no algorithm with a time or space bound. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper introduces no data structure with operations or costs. Tag clocks, stencils and decision trees are proof-time finite sets, not stored structures. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no circuit or parallel-execution content. Its recognizer is a deterministic finite automaton whose transition monoid is represented over a finite field, which is a proof device and not a hardware design. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Affine updates over a two-element field and residue clocks serve only to coordinate prefix and suffix tests on the same word. The paper states no hardness or security property. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Prediction trials are deterministic combinatorial guesses of monoid products, checked exactly. They involve no data, training or statistical error. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper gives an upper bound only. It does not state an objective to minimize and does not say whether a height below the bound is attainable. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The prefix and suffix tests of a split are independent languages on one word. They are not communicating processes, and the paper has no protocol, failure model or consensus content. |
| other (formal language theory and expression-height bookkeeping) | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | As a theorem it fixes a uniform nesting bound for generalized regular expressions with complement. That helps in reasoning about expression classes: a bounded nesting depth can be assumed for every regular language, but no size bound or minimality can. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-finite-alphabet-regular** (hypothesis): The bound of thirteen is proved for a finite alphabet and for languages accepted by a deterministic finite automaton. Nothing is claimed beyond that class. Blocked use: ['Applying the bound to languages over an infinite alphabet', 'Applying the bound to non-regular languages such as context-free or pushdown languages']
- **limit-complement-required** (hypothesis): The expressions use complement relative to the full word set. Earlier lower bounds for expressions without complement do not transfer, and the bound is not a statement about complement-free expressions. Blocked use: ['Reading the bound as a statement about ordinary regular expressions without complement', 'Using the bound for POSIX-style engines that lack complement']
- **limit-no-length-control** (formal_scope): The result bounds only the nesting of stars. Expression length is unrestricted, and the finite objects of the construction can be extremely large. Blocked use: ['Using the construction to obtain compact or polynomially sized expressions', 'Quoting a running time or size bound for the construction']
- **limit-not-minimal-height** (formal_scope): The theorem is an upper bound. It neither computes the minimal generalized height of a given language nor settles whether height one always suffices. Blocked use: ['Treating the bound as the exact star height of a language', 'Building a minimal star-height optimizer on this bound alone', 'Assuming that height one always suffices']
- **limit-prefix-code-parsing** (hypothesis): The split identity and the unique parsing rely on the episodes forming a prefix code. A word set in which one member is a proper prefix of another does not give unique factorization. Blocked use: ['Applying the split identity to a set of words that is not a prefix code']
- **limit-split-availability** (hypothesis): The split identity needs, for every episode outside the skipped class and every input state, a successful split into a prefix and a suffix test. Without this availability the graph test is not captured. Blocked use: ['Skipping the availability check at some episode', 'Treating soundness of the tests as sufficient without availability']
- **limit-clock-existence-only** (nonconstructive): The separating partitions of the tag clock are shown to exist by a probabilistic counting argument and are not given explicitly. The clock also needs every prime factor of the modulus to exceed the threshold. Blocked use: ['Presenting the tag clock as an explicit algorithm', 'Reusing the clock with a modulus that has a small prime factor']
- **limit-marker-separation** (hypothesis): The local marker rule needs the separation parameter and block length chosen first, and then gives only a marker-free window with a short period. Blocks that are too short give no periodic extension. Blocked use: ['Using marker-free windows as periodic when the block length is below the stated threshold']
- **limit-perfect-prediction** (edge_case): A block is guaranteed to be eligible for every input only when the trial is perfectly predicted. A trial without perfect prediction gives at most one eligible block, so another split is needed on that domain. Blocked use: ['Assuming every trial has an eligible block for every input']
- **limit-visibility-rejection** (edge_case): Every bounded inspection of a test must lie on its reading side. A test with an unavailable position or candidate rejects, and the position is never dropped from a guard. Blocked use: ['Omitting an unavailable candidate from a universal guard or uniqueness test']
- **limit-parameter-fixed-monoid** (formal_scope): The parameters are chosen for one fixed finite monoid, finite representation space and alphabet. The choice order matters, so the schedules are not reusable across different monoids. Blocked use: ['Reusing one parameter schedule for a different monoid or representation space']
- **limit-template-only** (hypothesis): The compilation to height one applies only after the input is restricted to bounded-prefix, periodic-run and bounded-tail templates. Arbitrary monoid-product tests are not made cheap. Blocked use: ['Applying the template lemma to unrestricted monoid-product tests']

>
> ## Scope
>
> Generalized star height measures the nesting of Kleene stars in regular expressions that also allow Boolean operations. The formalization proves that every regular language over a finite alphabet has a generalized expression over the same alphabet of star height at most three. This is stronger than the bound of thirteen in the accompanying finite-monoid paper. The selected statement asserts the uniform expression bound, without separately encoding every construction step of that paper.
>
> The formalization proves that every regular language over a finite alphabet has a generalized regular expression over that same alphabet with star height at most three. Generalized expressions allow Boolean operations as well as concatenation and Kleene star. The bound of three implies the accompanying paper's bound of four; the selected statement concerns the expression bound itself.
>
> Generalized star height measures the nesting of Kleene stars in regular expressions that also allow Boolean operations. The formalization proves that every regular language over a finite alphabet has a generalized regular expression over that same alphabet of star height at most three. Complements are taken in the same free monoid. This is the paper's uniform bound.

Comparator source `GeneralizedStarHeight.lean` (1368 bytes, sha256 `795709e34771f1c786ecae7c123e8a586c426d8e93c8b5bc82f2904d5340556e`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace GeneralizedStarHeight

universe u

inductive Expression (Alphabet : Type u) : Type u
  | zero : Expression Alphabet
  | one : Expression Alphabet
  | letter : Alphabet → Expression Alphabet
  | union : Expression Alphabet → Expression Alphabet → Expression Alphabet
  | concat : Expression Alphabet → Expression Alphabet → Expression Alphabet
  | compl : Expression Alphabet → Expression Alphabet
  | star : Expression Alphabet → Expression Alphabet

namespace Expression

open scoped Computability

def language {Alphabet : Type u} : Expression Alphabet → Language Alphabet
  | .zero => 0
  | .one => 1
  | .letter a => {[a]}
  | .union P Q => P.language + Q.language
  | .concat P Q => P.language * Q.language
  | .compl P => P.languageᶜ
  | .star P => P.language∗

def height {Alphabet : Type u} : Expression Alphabet → ℕ
  | .zero | .one | .letter _ => 0
  | .union P Q | .concat P Q => max P.height Q.height
  | .compl P => P.height
  | .star P => 1 + P.height

end Expression

def HasHeightAtMost {Alphabet : Type u} (L : Language Alphabet) (n : ℕ) : Prop :=
  ∃ P : Expression Alphabet, P.language = L ∧ P.height ≤ n

theorem main {Alphabet : Type u} [Finite Alphabet] (L : Language Alphabet) (hL : L.IsRegular) :
    HasHeightAtMost L 3 := by
  sorry

end GeneralizedStarHeight

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


Every regular language over a finite alphabet has a generalized regular expression of star height at most thirteen over that same alphabet. We prove this uniform bound by representing finite monoid computations as affine updates and recovering them through twelve successive split constructions.
